## Management Summary

### Verdict

🔴 User accounts, server processes, and the full customer database are all reachable by an unauthenticated attacker; several critical defects require no credentials, no configuration, and no prior access.

**Risk distribution:** 🔴 Critical: 6 · 🟠 High: 40 · 🟡 Medium: 22 · 🟢 Low: n/a · **Total: 68**<br/>**Reporting threshold:** medium - Low and Informational excluded<br/>**Assessment evidence:** 62 confirmed-exploitable finding(s) · 6 implementation weakness(es) · 7 design weakness(es)

Declared business impact: no material harm for 3 of 8 modeled components under the stated use-case assumptions. Finding severities and the verdict retain their technical security concern levels; evidence of consequences outside those assumptions still requires review.


**Method and limits:** Automated static analysis of code and configuration, not a pentest or a team threat-modeling session; CI/CD Pipeline was only screened - see [§1 Scope](#scope) and [§11 Out of Scope](#11-out-of-scope).

<br/>

**Security concerns behind this assessment:**

<blockquote style="border-left: 3px solid #dc2626; background: #fef2f2; padding: 16px 20px; margin: 0;">

- **Account takeover without credentials** — SQL injection and a hardcoded signing key let any internet visitor log in as any user, including admin. *(🔴 [F-003](#f-003) — SQL injection (`routes/login.ts:34`), 🟠 [F-011](#f-011) — Hardcoded RSA private key enables offline JWT forgery (`lib/insecurity.ts:23`) → [W-002](#w-002), [W-010](#w-010))* — ✓ cited finding in a code-verified chain
- **All customer data exfiltrated** — SQL injection in the product search returns every user's email and password hash in a single unauthenticated query. *(🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — SQL Injection in Product Search (`routes/search.ts:23`) → [W-002](#w-002))*
- **Self-promotion to administrator** — Any visitor can register with an admin role by adding a field to the sign-up request, bypassing all privilege controls. *(🔴 [F-008](#f-008) — Mass Assignment Allows Admin Role Injection (`routes/verify.ts:52`))* — ✓ cited finding in a code-verified chain
- **Remote server takeover** — A crafted profile update triggers server-side code evaluation, giving the attacker full control of the server process and all its data. *(🔴 [F-006](#f-006) — Server-Side Code Execution (`routes/userProfile.ts:61`) → [W-001](#w-001))* — ✓ cited finding in a code-verified chain
- **Any customer's account readable and writable** — Missing ownership checks let a signed-in attacker read or overwrite any other customer's records and product reviews. *(🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — Insecure Direct Object Reference, 🟠 [F-048](#f-048) — IDOR: review update without ownership check (`routes/updateProductReviews.ts:16`) → [W-003](#w-003))* — ✓ cited finding in a code-verified chain
- **Server files exposed via file upload** — The file-upload handler processes external document references without restriction, letting an unauthenticated attacker read any file on the server. *(🔴 [F-007](#f-007) — XXE (`routes/fileUpload.ts:83`))*
- **Open real-time channel and persistent stolen sessions** — The real-time channel admits anonymous connections, and a logged-out session token stays usable for six hours after logout. *(🟠 [F-016](#f-016) — Unauthenticated WebSocket Channel, 🟡 [F-071](#f-071) — UpdateAuthenticatedUsers re-inserts valid JWT bypassing (`lib/insecurity.ts:191`) → [W-004](#w-004))* — ✓ cited finding in a code-verified chain

</blockquote>

<br/>

Addressing these systemic gaps across authentication, data access, and file handling will substantially reduce the exposure to unauthenticated attackers and lateral movement within the application.

### Top Weaknesses

The systemic root-cause problems behind the findings - the classes to fix, not just individual bugs. Each links to the full [Weakness Register](#7-weakness-register) with its findings and remediation.

- 🔴 **[W-001](#w-001) - Application data crosses into executable code** (Critical) - An interpreter evaluates application values as code. _Proven by [F-006](#f-006)._
- 🔴 **[W-002](#w-002) - Database access relies on concatenated queries** (Critical) - Database queries are assembled from application values instead of passing those values through an enforced parameterised data-access path. _Proven by [F-003](#f-003), [F-005](#f-005)._
- 🔴 **[W-003](#w-003) - Authorization is implemented route by route** (Critical) - Authorization depends on per-handler checks instead of a policy boundary that consistently enforces role, ownership, and tenant scope. _Proven by [F-004](#f-004), [F-037](#f-037), [F-045](#f-045) (+3 more)._
- 🟠 **[W-004](#w-004) - Endpoints are reachable without enforced authentication** (High) - Sensitive API routes and real-time channels are exposed without an enforced authentication check at the endpoint boundary. _Proven by [F-016](#f-016), [F-049](#f-049)._
- 🟠 **[W-005](#w-005) - Input handling lacks enforced boundary validation** (High) - Request handlers do not validate input against one enforced server-side schema or allowlist at the boundary - validation is either absent on the vulnerable parameters or limited to rejecting select… _Proven by [F-022](#f-022), [F-026](#f-026)._
- 🟠 **[W-006](#w-006) - Document-database queries take their structure from application values** (High) - Document-store queries take operators or JavaScript predicates from application values instead of fixed query structures with typed values. _Proven by [F-001](#f-001)._

### Open Questions for the Team

The code cannot answer these questions; the people who own the business and the deployment can. Each question states under it what its answer changes.

- How critical are User Credentials and Password Hashes, User Personal Data PII and Payment Card Data to the business, and what harm would their disclosure, manipulation or unavailability cause?
  _The answer weights the impact rating and fix order of every finding that reaches these assets; recorded with the asset name and concrete harm in `docs/security/business-context.md`, the next full run applies it._
- Which cross-user or cross-tenant operations in Express\.js REST API Backend or CI/CD Pipeline are intended for support staff or administrators? ([W-003](#w-003): [F-004](#f-004), [F-037](#f-037), [F-045](#f-045) (unproven) +3 more)
  _Each access confirmed as intended narrows the finding to the remaining routes; if none are intended, every listed route is a live authorization gap._
- Which sensitive business records can the production database account used by Authentication & Session Surface or Express\.js REST API Backend access? ([W-002](#w-002): [F-003](#f-003), [F-005](#f-005))
  _Personal or regulated data in reach raises both the breach impact and any notification duty; a narrowly scoped account limits the blast radius._

### Security Posture & Top Threats

**Figure 1 - Architecture and Threat Overview**

Juice Shop has 8 components in 4 layers and exchanges data with 2 external services. Figure 1 shows these data flows and where each of the 8 attack scenarios below begins. The complete boundary catalogue remains in [§1 Trust Boundaries](#trust-boundaries).

![Figure 1 - Architecture and Threat Overview](threat-model-juice-shop-standard-v0.6.0b4.figure1.svg)

Role access and finding assignments are listed in [Identified Actors](#identified-actors).

**Actor grouping.** Regular self-registered users are grouped with anonymous internet attackers because registration is open. Each finding retains its login and privilege requirements.

**Figure 2 - Attack Routes and Impact**

Each numbered route names one example finding from the corresponding Top Threats group with its access prerequisite, the register weakness it is linked to, and the group's potential business harm, which depends on deployment and affected assets. W-IDs in parentheses link the example to an existing weakness. The attack step, affected component and technical consequence remain in the SVG tooltips and the findings register. Red arrows indicate attacks; dashed red arrows involve a victim; grey arrows lead to potential harm.

![Figure 2 - Actors, attack routes, weaknesses and impact](threat-model-juice-shop-standard-v0.6.0b4.figure2.svg)

**Threat actors.** The actors below drive the numbered attack paths in the figures above. The **Juice Shop User** is the *victim* of client-side attacks (XSS / CSRF), not an attacker. Figure 2 marks victim interaction on the applicable attack routes.

- **Juice Shop User** — legitimate end user; target of client-side attacks; target of ⑥ Output Encoding / Cross-Site Scripting, ⑦ CSRF / Permissive CORS.
- **Internet Attacker** — can self-register a regular account; drives ① Insecure Query Construction & Data Access, ② Hardcoded Secrets & Weak Cryptography, ③ Broken Authorization & Access Control, ④ Sensitive File & Secret Exposure, ⑤ Remote Code Execution (unsafe eval), ⑥ Output Encoding / Cross-Site Scripting, ⑦ CSRF / Permissive CORS.
- **Supply-Chain / Build Attacker** — compromises a dependency or build pipeline, or contributes malicious code to a public repo; drives ⑧ Build Pipeline & Dependency Integrity.

**8 structural threats**, grouped by weakness class - each row is one threat, not one finding. *Threat Description* states the general architectural weakness (STRIDE in brackets); *Findings* lists the concrete instances, each linked to [§8 Findings Register](#8-findings-register) with its component; *Risk & Impact* combines severity with business consequence.

Stored XSS (`last-login-ip.component.ts:39`) <span style="white-space:nowrap">→&nbsp;[C-03](#c-03)</span>&nbsp;Angular Web Application (SPA)<br/><span style="white-space:nowrap">🟡&nbsp;[F-056](#f-056)</span> - Challenge-gated weak sanitization stores XSS HTML in Users (`user.ts:50`) <span style="white-space:nowrap">→&nbsp;[C-02](#c-02)</span>&nbsp;Express\.js REST API Backend | 🟠 **High**<br/>Customer Session Hijack | <span style="white-space:nowrap">◕ [M-032](#m-032)</span> — Encode output instead of bypassing the framework sanitizer<br/><span style="white-space:nowrap">◕ [M-033](#m-033)</span> — Encode output instead of bypassing the framework sanitizer |
| <a id="path-cross-site-request-forgery"></a>⑦ | **CSRF / Permissive CORS** _(S·T)_<br/>a permissive CORS policy plus missing anti-CSRF tokens let any external page issue authenticated state-changing requests in the victim's session. | <span style="white-space:nowrap">🟠&nbsp;[F-009](#f-009)</span> - OAuth CSRF (`login.component.ts:152`) <span style="white-space:nowrap">→&nbsp;[C-03](#c-03)</span>&nbsp;Angular Web Application (SPA)<br/><span style="white-space:nowrap">🟡&nbsp;[F-055](#f-055)</span> - Wildcard CORS Permits Credentialed Cross-Origin Requests (`server.ts:183`) <span style="white-space:nowrap">→&nbsp;[C-02](#c-02)</span>&nbsp;Express\.js REST API Backend | 🟠 **High**<br/>Customer Session Hijack | <span style="white-space:nowrap">◕ [M-024](#m-024)</span> — Add anti-CSRF protection to state-changing requests<br/><span style="white-space:nowrap">◑ [M-068](#m-068)</span> — Restrict CORS to trusted origins |
| <a id="path-supply-chain"></a>⑧ | **Build Pipeline & Dependency Integrity** _(T·E)_<br/>CI workflows reference mutable action versions and run install scripts with elevated privileges, enabling a compromised dependency to execute code in the build pipeline. | <span style="white-space:nowrap">🟠&nbsp;[F-025](#f-025)</span> - _STRIDE: S spoofing · T tampering · R repudiation · I information disclosure · D denial of service · E elevation of privilege. Risk, findings, components, impact and Fix are derived deterministically; only the one-line weakness description is authored._

**Verified attack chains.** 6 fully viable ([AC-T-002](#ac-t-002), [AC-T-004](#ac-t-004), [AC-T-006](#ac-t-006), [AC-T-005](#ac-t-005), [AC-T-001](#ac-t-001), [AC-T-003](#ac-t-003)). These chains combine individual findings into end-to-end exploitation paths verified step-by-step against the code - see [§9 Abuse Cases](#9-abuse-cases) for the per-step breakdown and blocking mitigations.

### Top Mitigations

Highest-impact P1/P2 mitigations - 10 of 45 qualifying (79 total). Full detail in [§10 Mitigation Register](#10-mitigation-register). All 9 mitigation(s) that fix a Critical finding are always listed here.

| # | Component | Mitigation | Addresses | Effort |
|---|----------------------|------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------|------|
| **1** | [C-01](#c-01) — Authentication & Session Surface | ● [M-018](#m-018) — Use parameterized database queries (`login.ts:34`)<br/>*Declared business context* | 🔴 [F-003](#f-003) — SQL injection (`routes/login.ts`) | Low |
| **2** | [C-02](#c-02) — Express\.js REST API Backend | ● [M-020](#m-020) — Use parameterized database queries (`search.ts:23`) | 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — SQL Injection in Product Search (`routes/search.ts`) | Low |
| **3** | [C-02](#c-02) — Express\.js REST API Backend | ● [M-021](#m-021) — Remove server-side evaluation of untrusted input (`userProfile.ts:61`) | 🔴 [F-006](#f-006) — Server-Side Code Execution (`routes/userProfile.ts`) | Low |
| **4** | [C-02](#c-02) — Express\.js REST API Backend | ● [M-022](#m-022) — Disable XML external entity (XXE) resolution (`fileUpload.ts:83`) | 🔴 [F-007](#f-007) — XXE (`routes/fileUpload.ts`) | Low |
| **5** | [C-02](#c-02) — Express\.js REST API Backend | ● [M-023](#m-023) — Allowlist client-controlled fields (`verify.ts:52`) | 🔴 [F-008](#f-008) — Mass Assignment Allows Admin Role Injection (`routes/verify.ts`) | Low |
| **6** | [C-02](#c-02) — Express\.js REST API Backend | ● [M-019](#m-019) — Enforce object-level (ownership) authorization (`address.ts:18`) | 🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — Insecure Direct Object Reference (`routes/address.ts`) | Medium |
| **7** | [C-01](#c-01) — Authentication & Session Surface | ◕ [M-026](#m-026) — Move cryptographic keys to a managed secret store (`insecurity.ts:23`)<br/>*Declared business context* | 🟠 [F-011](#f-011) — Hardcoded RSA private key enables offline JWT forgery (`lib/insecurity.ts`) | Medium |
| **8** | [C-01](#c-01) — Authentication & Session Surface | ◕ [M-027](#m-027) — Enforce JWT signature and algorithm verification (`insecurity.ts:54`)<br/>*Declared business context* | 🟠 [F-012](#f-012) — Missing JWT algorithm whitelist (`lib/insecurity.ts`) | Low |
| **9** | [C-02](#c-02) — Express\.js REST API Backend | ◕ [M-061](#m-061) — Enforce object-level (ownership) authorization (`updateProductReviews.ts:16`) | 🟠 [F-048](#f-048) — IDOR: review update without ownership check (`routes/updateProductReviews.ts`) | Low |
| **10** | [C-06](#c-06) — Real-time WebSocket Channel | ◕ [M-031](#m-031) — Require authentication on every exposed endpoint (`registerWebsocketEvents.ts:24`) | 🟠 [F-016](#f-016) — Unauthenticated WebSocket Channel (`lib/startup/registerWebsocketEvents.ts`) | Medium |

*35 additional P1/P2 mitigations capped from the leader-board · 34 P3 backlog items in [§10 Mitigation Register](#10-mitigation-register). Sorted by priority (P1 first), then declared business context, then component, then leverage (most findings first), severity (Critical first), and effort (Low first).*

### AI / LLM Exposure

The backend chat route embeds an LLM that processes untrusted user messages without input guards or output filtering, exposing the application to policy bypass, sensitive data leakage, and resource exhaustion. See **[§6 Security Architecture](#6-security-architecture)** for the per-control detail.



- **[LLM01](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm01/) / [ASI01](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/) Prompt Injection** — User chat messages are concatenated into the system prompt without sanitization; an attacker can override the 10% discount cap in the system prompt and obtain a cryptographically valid coupon for any discount value, including 100%. _([C-02](#c-02) — Express\.js REST API Backend)_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-027](#f-027) — Prompt injection bypasses coupon discount policy (`chat.ts:177`)

- **[LLM02](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm02/) Sensitive Information Disclosure** — The chat route returns unfiltered order data including masked email addresses and product details to any caller who supplies a user identifier; an unverified token in the same route extends this to unauthenticated impersonation of any account. _([C-02](#c-02) — Express\.js REST API Backend)_
  - ↳ 🟡 [F-064](#f-064) — Chatbot returns unfiltered user order data (`chat.ts:164`)
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-015](#f-015) — Unverified token decode enables order impersonation (`chat.ts:45`)

- **[LLM10](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm10/) Unbounded Consumption** — The chat endpoint imposes no per-request token limit or rate limit, so an attacker can send unlimited large prompts to exhaust the upstream model quota and deny the service to legitimate users. _([C-02](#c-02) — Express\.js REST API Backend)_
  - ↳ 🟡 [F-067](#f-067) — Unbounded token consumption on chat endpoint (`chat.ts:207`)

## 1. System Overview

**Repository:** `https://github.com/juice-shop/juice-shop.git`

### Scope

This threat model covers 8 components of juice-shop: **Authentication & Session Surface**, **Express\.js REST API Backend**, **Angular Web Application (SPA)**, **SQLite Database (Sequelize ORM)**, **MarsDB Embedded Document Store**, **Real-time WebSocket Channel**, **Web3 / Wallet / NFT Surface**, **CI/CD Pipeline**.

**7 of 8** modeled components were analysed with full STRIDE; **CI/CD Pipeline** was only screened, a shorter pass without follow-up code checks per finding.

**Out of scope:** third-party hosted dependencies, browser runtime, operating-system kernel, and the underlying network infrastructure.

**Basis:** An automated, AI-assisted threat model built from the repository's source code and configuration - static analysis only, no dynamic or penetration testing. It describes the system as built, not as designed.

---

<a id="identified-actors"></a>
### Identified Actors

Figure 1 draws **Internet Attacker** and **Supply-Chain / Build Attacker** as attackers and **Juice Shop User** and **Juice Shop Admin** as legitimate roles of Juice Shop. Each row gives that actor's access and the findings attributed to it.

| Actor | Type | Access | Scenarios | Attributed findings |
|----------------------|---------------|----------------------|--------------|-------------------|
| A1 · Internet Attacker | Attacker | can self-register a regular account | ① ② ③ ④ ⑤ ⑥ ⑦ | 🔴 6 · 🟠 28 · 🟡 11 |
| A2 · Supply-Chain / Build Attacker | Attacker | compromises a dependency or build pipeline,<br/>or contributes malicious code to a public<br/>repo | ⑧ | 🟠 3 · 🟡 9 |
| Juice Shop User | Role (victim) | Human user operating a web browser to<br/>interact with the Juice Shop Angular SPA,<br/>including anonymous shoppers and registered<br/>users. | ⑥ ⑦ | - |
| Juice Shop Admin | Privileged role | Application role with elevated rights,<br/>evidenced by its access check. | - | - |

_Exposure, most exposed first: ⚠ Review (endpoints unresolved) · 🌐 External (reached from outside the model) · ◐ Unverified (crossing not confirmed) · 🔒 Internal (both ends modelled) · ↗ Egress (reaches outside the model)._

_Kind - the crossing mechanism (build pipeline, in-process, network), then after `·` what changes across it: data-origin, identity, operator, privilege, tenant. Shown alone, nothing changes._

_Conditions - **inbound**: validation, authentication, authorization · **outbound**: egress content, egress destination, response trust (replies are untrusted input) · **internal**: query construction (data never becomes program text), authentication, authorization._

_Verdict per condition - **broken**: a linked finding proves the gap · in doubt: related findings, none linked here · not examined: nothing bears on it. `N related` counts further findings on the same condition. Linked findings sit under the condition they break, or under _Unattributed_. A link raises effective severity only at a confirmed internet ingress; raw risk never changes._

_Identical on every row, so stated once here instead of in a column: source `detected` (derived from inspected repository evidence); status `resolved`. Any row that deviates is shown in the table._

```mermaid
    classDef admin    fill:#fef3c7,stroke:#b45309,color:#78350f,stroke-width:1.5px
    classDef sys      fill:#f2f2f2,stroke:#424242,color:#111,stroke-width:1.5px
    classDef ext      fill:#f2f2f2,stroke:#9e9e9e,color:#424242,stroke-dasharray:3 3,stroke-width:1px
    class A0 attacker
    class A1 attacker
    class R2 user
    class R3 admin
    class E0 ext
    class E1 ext
    class SYSTEM sys
```

**Key takeaway:** Juice Shop serves Juice Shop User and Juice Shop Admin and depends on Google OAuth Authorization Server and OpenAI-Compatible / Ollama LLM Provider; Internet Attacker and Supply-Chain / Build Attacker attack it.

### 2.2 Container Architecture

Where Juice Shop runs and what it is built on: the deployment environment the repository declares, the container, runtime and frameworks each component runs in, and the pipelines that build and publish it. Findings per component are in Figure 1.

<!-- detail-table -->
| Layer | What runs there |
|-----------------|----------------------|
| Deployment | Kubernetes · GitLab Auto Deploy<br/>(`.gitlab/auto-deploy-values.yaml`): Service<br/>:3000 (ingress and TLS from chart defaults;<br/>ClusterIP :3000 → 3000) → Pod |
| Container | built from `Dockerfile`: nodejs24-debian13 ·<br/>user 65532 · :3000; 🔴 image contains the<br/>whole repository, including `ctf.key`,<br/>encryptionkeys/; 🟠 base image<br/>nodejs24-debian13 floats (no version tag) |
| Process | Node\.js 24, one process: Express ^4.22.1 ([C-01](#c-01), [C-02](#c-02), [C-07](#c-07)) · Socket\.IO ^3.1.2 ([C-06](#c-06))<br/>key libraries: `express-jwt` 0.1.3 · jsonwebtoken 0.4.0 · sequelize ^6.37.3 · `body-parser` ^1.20.4 |
| Embedded stores | SQLite ^5.1.7 ([C-04](#c-04)) · MarsDB ^0.6.11 ([C-05](#c-05)), inside the same process |
| Client | Angular ^21.2.1 ([C-03](#c-03)), in the browser |
| Third parties | Google OAuth Authorization Server (called<br/>from the browser; 🟠 OAuth implicit flow) ·<br/>OpenAI-Compatible / Ollama LLM Provider |
| Build and release | GitHub Actions → Docker Hub: 🟠 4 of 24 actions not SHA-pinned<br/>GitLab CI → GitLab registry, Kubernetes (auto deploy): 🟠 test, dast disabled<br/>container build (`Dockerfile`): node:24 → nodejs24-debian13; 🟠 no lockfile: 185 version ranges resolved at build<br/>pipeline components [C-08](#c-08) |

**Key takeaway:** 4 server components and 2 embedded data stores run in one Node\.js 24 process in one pod. Weakens a control: image contains the whole repository, including `ctf.key`, encryptionkeys/.

## 3. Attack Walkthroughs

This section walks through how the highest-risk findings are exploited - one short walkthrough per Critical, each with attack steps, a focused sequence diagram, and the primary mitigation. How weaknesses combine toward the worst-case goal is in the [Critical Attack Tree](#critical-attack-tree); full per-finding context (severity rationale, assets, detection signals) is in the [§8 Findings Register](#8-findings-register).

### 3.1 Mass Assignment Allows Admin Role Injection in Express js REST API Backend

**Source:** 🔴 [F-008](#f-008) — `routes/verify.ts:52`

Severity **Critical** ([CWE-915](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/915.html)). STRIDE: Elevation of Privilege. See [§8 F-008](#f-008) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. Send POST `/api/Users` with body `{"email":"attacker@x.com","password":"p4ss","passwordRepeat":"p4ss","role":"admin"}` to the unauthenticated user registration endpoint.
2. The Finale middleware passes all body fields to Sequelize without field-level filtering, creating the user with role='admin' in the Users table.
3. Authenticate via POST `/rest/user/login` to receive a JWT encoding the admin role, granting full administrative API access.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: express-api - routes/verify.ts:52
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting routes/verify.ts line 52
    App->>App: Vulnerable branch executes without the missing control
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-915
    alt Current state — F-008
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-008
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-023 — Allowlist client-controlled fields
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-023](#m-023) (Allowlist client-controlled fields) lands, 🔴 [F-008](#f-008) — Mass Assignment Allows Admin Role Injection (`routes/verify.ts:52`) is exploitable at `routes/verify.ts:52` (Critical-severity, [CWE-915](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/915.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-023](#m-023) (Allowlist client-controlled fields)

### 3.2 Server-Side Code Execution in User Profile

**Source:** 🔴 [F-006](#f-006) — `routes/userProfile.ts:61`

Severity **Critical** ([CWE-94](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/94.html)). STRIDE: Tampering. See [§8 F-006](#f-006) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. Authenticate as any user and send a profile update request setting the username field to #{`process.mainModule.require('child_process').execSync('id').toString()`}.
2. Request GET `/profile` while authenticated; the `getUserProfile` handler matches the #{...} pattern at line 54 and calls `eval(code)` at line 61.
3. The Node\.js process executes the injected expression with full server privileges and the result is interpolated into the HTML response.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant API
    participant Sandbox
    Note over API: express-api - routes/userProfile.ts:61
    Note over Sandbox: Eval / VM context
    Attacker->>API: GET /profile with JS payload escaping the sandbox
    API->>Sandbox: Pass attacker-controlled code to eval / vm.runInContext
    Sandbox->>Sandbox: Prototype-walk reaches the host realm - RCE
    API-->>Attacker: Command output / arbitrary side effects
    alt Current state — F-006
        Attacker->>API: The attacker sends the exploit for F-006
        API-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-021 — Remove server-side evaluation of untrusted input
        Attacker->>API: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        API-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-021](#m-021) (Remove server-side evaluation of untrusted input) lands, 🔴 [F-006](#f-006) — Server-Side Code Execution (`routes/userProfile.ts:61`) is exploitable at `routes/userProfile.ts:61` (Critical-severity, [CWE-94](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/94.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-021](#m-021) (Remove server-side evaluation of untrusted input)

### 3.3 Insecure Direct Object Reference in Address

**Source:** 🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — `routes/address.ts:18`

Severity **Critical** ([CWE-639](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/639.html)). STRIDE: Tampering. See [§8 F-004](#f-004) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. An attacker crafts a request targeting the weak spot at `routes/address.ts:18`.
2. A signed-in attacker puts another user's ID into the owner field of the request (`req.body.UserId` and the like); the query trusts it instead of the session identity, so the attacker reads or changes every other user's records - horizontal privilege escalation across all accounts.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: express-api - routes/address.ts:18
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting routes/address.ts line 18
    App->>App: Vulnerable branch executes without the missing control
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-639
    alt Current state — F-004
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-004
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-019 — Enforce object-level (ownership) authorization
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-019](#m-019) (Enforce object-level (ownership) authorization) lands, 🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — Insecure Direct Object Reference is exploitable at `routes/address.ts:18` (Critical-severity, [CWE-639](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/639.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-019](#m-019) (Enforce object-level (ownership) authorization)

### 3.4 SQL injection in Login

**Source:** 🔴 [F-003](#f-003) — `routes/login.ts:34`

Severity **Critical** ([CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)). STRIDE: Tampering. See [§8 F-003](#f-003) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. Attacker sends POST `/rest/user/login` with body `{"email":"' OR '1'='1'--","password":"x"}`, exploiting the absence of parameterized binding at `routes/login.ts:34`.
2. The injected SQL causes the WHERE clause to evaluate to TRUE for every row; Sequelize `plain:true` returns the first user record, typically the admin account.
3. The server issues a valid JWT for the returned user and stores the session in the in-memory token map, completing authentication bypass.
4. Attacker uses the issued JWT to call all authenticated API endpoints as the hijacked user.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant API
    participant DB
    Note over API: auth - routes/login.ts:34
    Note over DB: Database
    Attacker->>API: POST /rest/user/login with classical OR 1=1 payload
    API->>DB: SQL built by string interpolation - payload becomes query
    DB-->>API: First matching row regardless of intended predicate
    API-->>Attacker: 200 OK with authenticated session / leaked rows
    alt Current state — F-003
        Attacker->>API: The attacker sends the exploit for F-003
        API-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-018 — Use parameterized database queries
        Attacker->>API: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        API-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-018](#m-018) (Use parameterized database queries) lands, 🔴 [F-003](#f-003) — SQL injection (`routes/login.ts:34`) is exploitable at `routes/login.ts:34` (Critical-severity, [CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-018](#m-018) (Use parameterized database queries)

### 3.5 SQL Injection in Product Search

**Source:** 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — `routes/search.ts:23`

Severity **Critical** ([CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)). STRIDE: Tampering. See [§8 F-005](#f-005) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. Send GET `/rest/products/search?q=test`')) `UNION SELECT id,email,password,role,NULL,NULL,NULL,NULL FROM Users`-- to the unauthenticated endpoint.
2. The interpolated query returns all user rows merged with the product result set.
3. Parse the JSON response to extract all user emails and `MD5` password hashes for offline cracking.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant API
    participant DB
    Note over API: express-api - routes/search.ts:23
    Note over DB: Database
    Attacker->>API: GET /rest/products/search with classical OR 1=1 payload
    API->>DB: SQL built by string interpolation - payload becomes query
    DB-->>API: First matching row regardless of intended predicate
    API-->>Attacker: 200 OK with authenticated session / leaked rows
    alt Current state — F-005
        Attacker->>API: The attacker sends the exploit for F-005
        API-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-020 — Use parameterized database queries
        Attacker->>API: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        API-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-020](#m-020) (Use parameterized database queries) lands, 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — SQL Injection in Product Search (`routes/search.ts:23`) is exploitable at `routes/search.ts:23` (Critical-severity, [CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-020](#m-020) (Use parameterized database queries)

### 3.6 XXE in File Upload

**Source:** 🔴 [F-007](#f-007) — `routes/fileUpload.ts:83`

Severity **Critical** ([CWE-611](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/611.html)). STRIDE: Tampering. See [§8 F-007](#f-007) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. Craft an XML file containing <!DOCTYPE foo [<!ENTITY xxe SYSTEM "file:///etc/passwd">]>`<foo>`&xxe;</foo>.
2. Upload the file to POST `/file-upload` (no authentication required per `server.ts` line 309) with `Content-Type` multipart/form-data.
3. Read the 410 error response body which includes the parsed XML string containing the contents of `/etc/passwd`.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: express-api - routes/fileUpload.ts:83
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting routes/fileUpload.ts line 83
    App->>App: Vulnerable branch executes without the missing control
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-611
    alt Current state — F-007
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-007
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-022 — Disable XML external entity (XXE) resolution
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-022](#m-022) (Disable XML external entity (XXE) resolution) lands, 🔴 [F-007](#f-007) — XXE (`routes/fileUpload.ts:83`) is exploitable at `routes/fileUpload.ts:83` (Critical-severity, [CWE-611](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/611.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-022](#m-022) (Disable XML external entity (XXE) resolution)

### 3.7 Hardcoded RSA private key enables offline JWT forgery

**Source:** 🟠 [F-011](#f-011) — `lib/insecurity.ts:23`

Severity **High** ([CWE-321](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/321.html)). STRIDE: Spoofing. See [§8 F-011](#f-011) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. Attacker reads the full RSA private key literal from `lib/insecurity.ts:23` in the publicly available repository.
2. Attacker calls `jwt.sign({data:{role:'admin',id:<uid>,email:'admin@juice-sh.op'}}, privateKey, {algorithm:'RS256'})` offline.
3. Attacker sets the forged token in the `Authorization` header and sends requests to any JWT-protected endpoint.
4. All protected routes accept the token as authentic and grant admin-level access indefinitely until the key is rotated.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant Repo
    participant App
    Note over Repo: Public repository
    Note over App: auth - lib/insecurity.ts:23
    Attacker->>Repo: Clone or browse - extract private key from lib/insecurity.ts line 23
    Attacker->>Attacker: Sign forged token / signature with the extracted key
    Attacker->>App: Submit forged artefact in normal authentication flow
    App-->>Attacker: Request accepted as authentic - signature validates
    alt Current state — F-011
        Attacker->>Repo: The attacker sends the exploit for F-011
        Repo-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-026 — Move cryptographic keys to a managed secret store
        Attacker->>Repo: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        Repo-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ◕ [M-026](#m-026) (Move cryptographic keys to a managed secret store) lands, 🟠 [F-011](#f-011) — Hardcoded RSA private key enables offline JWT forgery (`lib/insecurity.ts:23`) is exploitable at `lib/insecurity.ts:23` (High-severity, [CWE-321](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/321.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ◕ [M-026](#m-026) (Move cryptographic keys to a managed secret store)

### 3.8 Unauthenticated WebSocket Channel in Real-time WebSocket Channel

**Source:** 🟠 [F-016](#f-016) — `lib/startup/registerWebsocketEvents.ts:24`

Severity **High** ([CWE-306](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/306.html)). STRIDE: Spoofing. See [§8 F-016](#f-016) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. An attacker crafts a request targeting the weak spot at `lib/startup/registerWebsocketEvents.ts:24`.
2. The attacker sends it; the missing control never rejects the crafted input.
3. An anonymous internet client opens a Socket\.IO connection on port 3000 without presenting any JWT or session credential.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: realtime-channel - lib/startup/registerWebsocketEvents.ts:24
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting lib/startup/registerWebsocketEvents.ts line 24
    App->>App: Vulnerable branch executes without the missing control
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-306
    alt Current state — F-016
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-016
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-031 — Require authentication on every exposed endpoint
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ◕ [M-031](#m-031) (Require authentication on every exposed endpoint) lands, 🟠 [F-016](#f-016) — Unauthenticated WebSocket Channel is exploitable at `lib/startup/registerWebsocketEvents.ts:24` (High-severity, [CWE-306](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/306.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ◕ [M-031](#m-031) (Require authentication on every exposed endpoint)

## 6. Security Architecture

This chapter is organized by security-control category. The architecture section avoids artificial control IDs and finding-ID columns in overview tables. Findings are listed only where the affected control is described.

_[§6](#6-security-architecture) schema v2 (13-section control-category layout). Cataloged controls: 27 total - 2 adequate, 7 partial, 5 weak, 9 unsafe, 4 missing. Linked threats: 71._

**How to read the verdicts.** Every control category (and every sub-control below it) carries exactly one status. The two red verdicts do **not** mean the same thing - this is the distinction that decides what you have to do about a finding:

| Status | Meaning | What it asks of you |
|----------|------------------------------------|------------------------|
| 🟢 Adequate | Control is present and sound | Nothing - keep it |
| 🟡 Partial | Present, but with meaningful gaps | Close the gap |
| 🟠 Weak | Present, but has exploitable gaps | Strengthen it |
| 🔴 Unsafe | **Present and relied upon, but defeated /<br/>trivially bypassable** | **Fix the existing control** |
| 🔴 Missing | **Control was never built** | **Add the control** |
| - | Not applicable to this codebase | - |

So "🔴 Unsafe" on a control category does *not* mean the control is absent - it means the control exists but does not hold (e.g. an `MD5` password hash, a raw-SQL query path, a hardcoded signing key). "🔴 Missing" is reserved for controls that were never built (e.g. no `Content-Security-Policy` header).

**Controls covered:**

- [6.2.1 Multi-Factor Authentication](#621-multi-factor-authentication)
- [6.2.2 Password-Based Login](#622-password-based-login)
- [6.2.3 User Registration](#623-user-registration)
- [6.2.4 Password Reset](#624-password-reset)

**Implemented controls:** `routes/2fa.ts:31`; `routes/login.ts:37-46`; `server.ts:309`,312,401-420; `lib/insecurity.ts:54`; `routes/login.ts:34`; `models/index.ts:34-41`; `server.ts:342-348`; Detected in scope: POST `/rest/user/login`.

**Assessment:** The identity surface spans password login, TOTP, password reset, social login, and open registration. SQL injection on the login query at `routes/login.ts:34` and a predictable password derived from the OAuth email at `oauth.component.ts:30` each allow account takeover without valid credentials. TOTP at `routes/2fa.ts:31` is correctly wired but the verification endpoint accepts unlimited guesses. Password reset at `routes/resetPassword.ts:10` relies on an unguessability assumption about security-question answers and applies no lockout.

<a id="multi-factor-authentication"></a><a id="multi-factor-authentication-totp"></a>
#### 6.2.1 Multi-Factor Authentication

**Status:** 🟢 Adequate - TOTP enrollment and verification at `routes/2fa.ts` are correctly wired to the login flow, but the verification endpoint accepts unlimited guesses against the 6-digit code space.

`routes/2fa.ts:31`; `routes/login.ts:37-46`

The TOTP verification flow on the successful path proceeds as follows:

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant C as Client
    participant S as Server (routes/2fa.ts)
    participant DB as Users table
    C->>S: POST /rest/2fa/verify {tmpToken, totp}
    S->>DB: Lookup TOTP secret for tmpToken holder
    DB-->>S: TOTP secret
    S->>S: Validate TOTP code (30s window)
    S-->>C: 200 {authentication: {token, bid}}
```

**Security assessment**

`routes/2fa.ts:31` validates a time-based one-time password against the per-user secret before issuing a full session token, correctly separating the TOTP step from the initial credential check at `routes/login.ts:37-46`. The verification endpoint (`POST /rest/2fa/verify`) applies no rate limit: a caller with a valid `tmpToken` can enumerate all 1,000,000 possible 6-digit codes. At 1,000 requests per second the entire space is exhausted in under 17 minutes.

**Relevant findings**

- 🟠 [F-012](#f-012) — Missing JWT algorithm whitelist
- 🟠 [F-013](#f-013) — Password reset accepts a security-question answer
- 🟠 [F-014](#f-014) — Rate Limit Bypass

<a id="route-authentication-middleware"></a>
**Route Authentication Middleware.**


**Status:** 🟠 Weak - middleware exported from `lib/insecurity.ts:54` guards most API routes but at least 20 sensitive routes in `server.ts` are registered outside the guarded block and receive no token check.

`server.ts:309`,312,401-420; `lib/insecurity.ts:54`

**Security assessment**

`lib/insecurity.ts:54` exports the `security.isAuthorized` middleware, which validates the Bearer token using `express-jwt`. `server.ts:309` and `server.ts:312` install this middleware, and it is applied to routes registered between lines 401 and 420. Routes registered outside that window - including `/support/logs` at `server.ts:281`, `/encryptionkeys` at line 277, and several admin-panel proxies around line 595 - are reachable without a valid token. The client-side `AdminGuard` at `frontend/src/app/app.guard.ts:52` adds a UI redirect but provides no server-side enforcement.

**Relevant findings**

- 🟠 [F-012](#f-012) — Missing JWT algorithm whitelist
- 🟠 [F-013](#f-013) — Password reset accepts a security-question answer
- 🟠 [F-014](#f-014) — Rate Limit Bypass

<a id="sql-query-parameterization"></a>
**SQL Query Parameterization.**


**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - the login query at `routes/login.ts:34` concatenates `req.body.email` directly into a raw `sequelize.query()` call, bypassing parameterization entirely and allowing SQL injection to authenticate as any user.

`routes/login.ts:34`; `models/index.ts:34-41`

**Security assessment**

`models/index.ts:34-41` configures Sequelize as the ORM layer and most model-level reads use parameterized finders. The login route diverges: `routes/login.ts:34` calls `models.sequelize.query()` with `req.body.email` interpolated into the SQL string. Submitting `' OR 1=1--` as the email returns the first row in the `Users` table, which is the seeded admin account, without requiring a matching password. A second raw query at `routes/search.ts:23` interpolates the search term into a `LIKE` clause with the same pattern.

**Relevant findings**

- 🟠 [F-012](#f-012) — Missing JWT algorithm whitelist
- 🟠 [F-013](#f-013) — Password reset accepts a security-question answer
- 🟠 [F-014](#f-014) — Rate Limit Bypass

<a id="rate-limiting"></a>
**Rate Limiting.**


**Status:** 🟡 Partial - rate limiting is configured on the login endpoint but keyed on a header that callers can spoof, and the TOTP verification endpoint has no limit at all.

Rate limiting protects accounts from automated credential-stuffing and brute-force attacks by capping how many attempts a single source may make within a time window. Juice Shop applies rate-limit middleware to the login and some authentication endpoints.

**Security assessment**

`server.ts:342-348` installs rate-limit middleware keyed on `req.headers['x-forwarded-for'] || req.ip`. Setting a spoofed `X-Forwarded-For` header rotates the per-source counter on each request, making the limit ineffective. `routes/2fa.ts:31` registers the TOTP verification endpoint with no rate-limit middleware at all, leaving the 6-digit code space open to exhaustion.

**Relevant findings**

- 🟠 [F-012](#f-012) — Missing JWT algorithm whitelist
- 🟠 [F-013](#f-013) — Password reset accepts a security-question answer
- 🟠 [F-014](#f-014) — Rate Limit Bypass

<a id="password-based-login"></a>
#### 6.2.2 Password-Based Login

**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - SQL injection on the login query makes authentication bypassable without a valid password.

Detected in scope: POST `/rest/user/login`

The intended password-login flow on the successful path:

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant C as Client
    participant S as Server (routes/login.ts)
    participant DB as SQLite (Sequelize)
    C->>S: POST /rest/user/login {email, password}
    S->>DB: SELECT user WHERE email = ? (parameterized - intended)
    DB-->>S: User row
    S->>S: Compare MD5(password) to stored hash
    S-->>C: 200 {authentication: {token, bid}}
```

**Security assessment**

Two independent weaknesses converge on the login path:

- `routes/login.ts:34` builds the user-lookup query by interpolating `req.body.email` into `models.sequelize.query()` - submitting `' OR 1=1--` as the email returns the seeded admin row without a matching password.
- Passwords are hashed with unsalted `MD5` at `lib/insecurity.ts:43`. A dump of the `Users` table yields hashes crackable against standard rainbow tables in seconds.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-003](#f-003) — SQL injection
- 🟠 [F-009](#f-009) — OAuth CSRF
- 🟠 [F-010](#f-010) — Predictable password derived from Google OAuth email
- 🟠 [F-013](#f-013) — Password reset accepts a security-question answer
- 🟠 [F-030](#f-030) — Hardcoded testing credentials in production bundle

<a id="user-registration"></a>
#### 6.2.3 User Registration

**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - the registration endpoint accepts a caller-supplied `role` field that is passed to the Sequelize model without stripping, allowing any visitor to create an admin-role account.

Detected in scope: POST `/api/Users`

The intended registration flow on the successful path:

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant C as Client
    participant S as Server (server.ts:407-421)
    participant DB as Users table
    C->>S: POST /api/Users {email, password, ...}
    S->>S: Validate email format (server.ts:407-418)
    S->>DB: INSERT user record
    DB-->>S: Created user row
    S-->>C: 201 {status: 'success', data: {id, email}}
```

**Security assessment**

`server.ts:407-421` exposes `POST /api/Users` without authentication. The endpoint is backed by the `finale` REST library via `routes/verify.ts:52`, which maps all request body fields directly to Sequelize model columns. Including `"role":"admin"` in the registration body creates an account with admin privileges. No field-strip or allowlist is applied to the incoming body before the model write.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-008](#f-008) — Mass Assignment Allows Admin Role Injection
- 🟠 [F-028](#f-028) — Client-Side Challenge Enforcement
- 🟠 [F-038](#f-038) — Unauthenticated CTF Flag Disclosure
- 🟠 [F-043](#f-043) — Unbounded WebSocket Connection Consumption
- 🟠 [F-045](#f-045) — Sensitive Routes Registered Without Authentication Middleware

<a id="password-reset"></a>
#### 6.2.4 Password Reset

**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - security-question answer is the sole verification factor with no per-account attempt lockout, allowing unlimited guessing.

Detected in scope: POST `/rest/user/reset-password`

The intended password-reset flow on the successful path:

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant C as Client
    participant S as Server (routes/resetPassword.ts)
    participant DB as SecurityAnswers table
    C->>S: POST /rest/user/reset-password {email, answer, newPassword}
    S->>DB: Lookup HMAC(answer) for email
    DB-->>S: Stored answer hash
    S->>S: Compare HMAC(submitted answer) to stored hash
    S->>DB: UPDATE password for user
    S-->>C: 200 {message: 'password_reset_successful'}
```

**Security assessment**

`routes/resetPassword.ts:10` accepts `{email, answer, newPassword}` and validates by comparing an HMAC digest of the submitted answer against the stored hash at `routes/resetPassword.ts:41`. No attempt counter or account lockout exists. Security-question answers (birthplace, mother's maiden name, favourite pet) are frequently guessable from public social media profiles. A caller who knows the target's email can submit guesses in a loop until `POST /rest/user/reset-password` succeeds and the account password is overwritten.

**Relevant findings**

- 🟠 [F-013](#f-013) — Password reset accepts a security-question answer
- 🟠 [F-044](#f-044) — Security-question password reset without lockout

<a id="social-login"></a><a id="social-login-oauth-oidc"></a>
#### 6.2.5 Social Login

**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - the OAuth callback at `login.component.ts:152` omits the `state` parameter, and the resulting account password is deterministically derived from the user's public email address.

Detected in scope: frontend/src/app/oauth

The intended OAuth login flow on the successful path:

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant U as User
    participant SPA as Angular (login.component.ts)
    participant Google as Google OAuth
    participant S as Server (routes/login.ts)
    U->>SPA: Click 'Login with Google'
    SPA->>Google: Redirect to authorization endpoint (no state param)
    Google-->>SPA: id_token in URL fragment
    SPA->>S: POST /rest/user/login {oauth: true, token: id_token}
    S->>S: Derive account password from email
    S-->>SPA: 200 {authentication: {token, bid}}
```

**Security assessment**

Two independent weaknesses undermine the OAuth flow:

- `login.component.ts:152` processes the Google OAuth redirect without generating or validating a `state` parameter - any page that can navigate the victim's browser to the callback URL completes the login on their behalf (CSRF).
- `oauth.component.ts:30` derives the account password by hashing the user's Google-provided email address with a predictable algorithm. Any caller who knows the victim's email can compute the same hash and authenticate directly via `POST /rest/user/login` without ever touching OAuth.

**Relevant findings**

- 🟠 [F-009](#f-009) — OAuth CSRF
- 🟠 [F-010](#f-010) — Predictable password derived from Google OAuth email

### 6.3 Session and Token Controls

<a id="ctrl-session-and-token-controls"></a>

**Dependent crossings:** [tb-1](#tb-1) refuted - 🟠 [F-012](#f-012) — Missing JWT algorithm whitelist, 🟠 [F-049](#f-049) — Missing authentication on key submission endpoint (`routes/checkKeys.ts:6`), 🟡 [F-050](#f-050) — Open Redirect (`routes/redirect.ts:19`) · [tb-2](#tb-2) unconfirmed - 🟡 [F-058](#f-058) — Missing Container Image Signing


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-011](#w-011)
**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.3.1 Session Token Validation](#631-session-token-validation)
- [6.3.2 Session and Token Hardening](#632-session-and-token-hardening)

**Implemented controls:** `lib/insecurity.ts:54-57`; `routes/login.ts:24-26`; `lib/insecurity.ts:72-93`; `server.ts:289`; `lib/insecurity.ts:56`,72-93; `frontend/src/app/Services/request.interceptor.ts:13`.

**Assessment:** JWTs are issued by `lib/insecurity.ts:72-93` using an RSA private key and returned in the login response. The signing key is hardcoded in source at `lib/insecurity.ts:23`, no algorithm allowlist constrains the `alg` header a caller can supply, and tokens are stored in `localStorage` rather than an `HttpOnly` cookie - making them accessible to any injected script. Token revocation is broken: `lib/insecurity.ts:191` re-inserts a presented token into the valid-token map on every authenticated request, so a token removed from the map is reinstated on the holder's next request.

<a id="session-token-validation"></a><a id="session-token-validation-jwt-based"></a>
#### 6.3.1 Session Token Validation

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - JWT verification runs on each request via `express-jwt` but accepts any algorithm the caller names in the token header, enabling algorithm-confusion forgery.

`lib/insecurity.ts:54-57`; `routes/login.ts:24-26`; `lib/insecurity.ts:72-93`

**Security assessment**

`lib/insecurity.ts:54-57` calls `expressjwt()` to verify incoming Bearer tokens. The options object contains no `algorithms:` array, so the library trusts the `alg` field in the attacker-controlled JWT header. An attacker who downloads the RSA public key from the unauthenticated `/encryptionkeys` endpoint can forge a token by signing it with `alg:HS256` using the public key bytes as the HMAC secret - the server accepts this forgery as a valid session token for any identity in the payload.

**Relevant findings**

- 🟠 [F-031](#f-031) — JWT stored in localStorage (JWT stored in `localStorage`)
- 🟡 [F-071](#f-071) — UpdateAuthenticatedUsers re-inserts valid JWT bypassing

<a id="session-and-token-hardening"></a>
#### 6.3.2 Session and Token Hardening

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - token storage is in `localStorage`, revocation is defeated by an unconditional re-insertion, and no expiry enforcement prevents indefinitely-valid tokens.

`server.ts:289`; `lib/insecurity.ts:56`,72-93; `frontend/src/app/Services/request.interceptor.ts:13`

**Security assessment**

`login.component.ts:105` writes the session JWT to `localStorage`, making it readable by any JavaScript executing in the same origin - a single successful XSS payload extracts the token. `lib/insecurity.ts:191` is called via `updateAuthenticatedUsers` on every authenticated request: it re-inserts the presented token into the in-memory token map, so a logout that removes the token from that map is immediately reversed when the same token is used again. `frontend/src/app/Services/request.interceptor.ts:13` attaches the `Authorization` header to every outbound HTTP request regardless of destination path.

**Relevant findings**

- 🟠 [F-031](#f-031) — JWT stored in localStorage (JWT stored in `localStorage`)
- 🟡 [F-071](#f-071) — UpdateAuthenticatedUsers re-inserts valid JWT bypassing

The JWT lifecycle from issuance through browser storage to bearer presentation follows this flow:

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant C as Client (login.component.ts:105)
    participant S as Server (lib/insecurity.ts)
    C->>S: POST /rest/user/login
    S->>S: Sign JWT with RSA key (lib/insecurity.ts:72-93)
    S-->>C: 200 {token}
    C->>C: localStorage.setItem('token', token)
    C->>S: GET /api/resource  Authorization: Bearer {token}
    S->>S: express-jwt verifies token (lib/insecurity.ts:54)
    S-->>C: 200 resource response
```

### 6.4 Authorization Controls

<a id="ctrl-authorization-controls"></a>

**Dependent crossings:** [tb-1](#tb-1) refuted - 🔴 [F-008](#f-008) — Mass Assignment Allows Admin Role Injection (`routes/verify.ts:52`) · [tb-2](#tb-2) unconfirmed - 🟠 [F-046](#f-046) — Missing permissions block default GITHUB_TOKEN scope, 🟡 [F-060](#f-060) — Default GITHUB_TOKEN Scope Not Restricted


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-003](#w-003), [W-007](#w-007)
**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.4.1 Centralized Route Authorization](#641-centralized-route-authorization)
- [6.4.2 Object-Level Access Control](#642-object-level-access-control)
- [6.4.3 Management Endpoint Protection](#643-management-endpoint-protection)

**Implemented controls:** `server.ts:350-640`; `server.ts:602`,425,432,439; `server.ts:362-395`.

**Assessment:** Route authorization in `server.ts:350-640` relies on `security.isAuthorized` from `lib/insecurity.ts:54` applied to a subset of routes. Admin-only operations check `req.user.data.role === 'admin'` inline at `server.ts:362-395`. Object-level ownership is unenforced on most data-mutation routes - the authenticated user's ID is not compared against the resource owner on addresses, basket items, payment methods, or orders. A client-side `AdminGuard` at `frontend/src/app/app.guard.ts:52` adds a UI gate backed by no server-side role check.

<a id="centralized-route-authorization"></a>
#### 6.4.1 Centralized Route Authorization

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - middleware guards most authenticated endpoints but a significant set of sensitive routes in `server.ts` are registered outside the guarded block and receive no token verification.

Authenticated users access protected API endpoints only after the server validates their Bearer token. The JWT middleware runs before the route handler and rejects requests without a valid token, preventing unauthenticated access to account data, order history, and shopping baskets.

**Security assessment**

`lib/insecurity.ts:54` exports the JWT verification middleware applied to routes registered between `server.ts:401` and `server.ts:420`. Routes registered outside that window - including `/support/logs` at line 281, `/encryptionkeys` at line 277, and multiple admin-facing endpoints around lines 595–640 - are not covered by the middleware and are reachable without a valid token. The Angular `AdminGuard` at `frontend/src/app/app.guard.ts:52` performs a client-side-only redirect that any caller bypassing the SPA can ignore.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — Insecure Direct Object Reference
- 🔴 [F-008](#f-008) — Mass Assignment Allows Admin Role Injection
- 🟠 [F-028](#f-028) — Client-Side Challenge Enforcement

<a id="object-level-access-control"></a><a id="object-level-access-control-bolaidor"></a>
#### 6.4.2 Object-Level Access Control

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - authenticated callers can operate on any user's resources by supplying the target's numeric ID; no handler compares `req.user.data.id` against the resource owner.

`server.ts:602`,425,432,439

**Security assessment**

Data-mutation routes for addresses (`routes/address.ts:18,29`), payment methods (`routes/payment.ts:21,41,70`), orders (`routes/order.ts:144,146,153`), and wallet balance (`routes/wallet.ts:12,24,27`) each accept a user-supplied ID without verifying that the authenticated caller owns the referenced object. An authenticated user who increments or substitutes the target's numeric ID in the URL path can read, modify, or delete another user's resources. `server.ts:602,604` exposes admin-only product mutation routes that perform the same pattern.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — Insecure Direct Object Reference
- 🔴 [F-008](#f-008) — Mass Assignment Allows Admin Role Injection
- 🟠 [F-028](#f-028) — Client-Side Challenge Enforcement

<a id="management-endpoint-protection"></a>
#### 6.4.3 Management Endpoint Protection

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - inline role checks protect some admin endpoints but are absent from others, and the check itself relies on a bearer token whose signature key is publicly known.

Management operations such as updating product listings and accessing the admin panel require the caller to hold an admin-role JWT. The server enforces this by checking `req.user.data.role === 'admin'` before allowing access to restricted operations.

**Security assessment**

`server.ts:362-395` applies inline role checks to product administration routes. The check is absent from `/support/logs` at line 281 and several management-surface routes registered after line 595. Because the RSA private key is hardcoded at `lib/insecurity.ts:23` and committed to the public repository, any caller can forge an admin-role token with a standard JWT library and bypass the role check on endpoints that do implement it.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — Insecure Direct Object Reference
- 🔴 [F-008](#f-008) — Mass Assignment Allows Admin Role Injection
- 🟠 [F-028](#f-028) — Client-Side Challenge Enforcement

### 6.5 Query Construction and Data Access Controls

<a id="ctrl-query-construction-and-data-access-controls"></a>

**Dependent crossings:** [tb-4](#tb-4) refuted - 🔴 [F-003](#f-003) — SQL injection (`routes/login.ts:34`), 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — SQL Injection in Product Search (`routes/search.ts:23`) · [tb-5](#tb-5) refuted - 🟠 [F-001](#f-001) — NoSQL Injection in MarsDB Query Layer


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-002](#w-002), [W-006](#w-006)
**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the section's default mechanism — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.5.1 Database Query Construction](#651-database-query-construction)

**Implemented controls:** Sequelize ORM parameterized finders on most data routes; `Number()` coercion on the non-challenge MarsDB path in `routes/showProductReviews.ts`.

**Assessment:** Sequelize ORM finders parameterize the majority of queries and prevent SQL injection on most routes. Two routes diverge to raw `sequelize.query()` calls with string interpolation (`routes/login.ts:34` and `routes/search.ts:23`), and `routes/showProductReviews.ts:36` concatenates a route parameter directly into a MarsDB `$where` JavaScript expression.

<a id="database-query-construction"></a><a id="query-construction"></a>
#### 6.5.1 Database Query Construction

**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - raw Sequelize queries at two routes allow full SQL injection, and a MarsDB `$where` concatenation allows JavaScript injection into the NoSQL query engine.

Parameterized queries keep user-supplied values as data and prevent them from being interpreted as SQL syntax. Sequelize ORM finders used on most routes pass values as bound parameters, maintaining a clean separation between query structure and input data.

**Security assessment**

Two SQL injection paths exist alongside the ORM layer:

- `routes/login.ts:34` calls `models.sequelize.query()` with `req.body.email` interpolated directly - `' OR 1=1--` returns the seeded admin row.
- `routes/search.ts:23` interpolates the `q` parameter into a `LIKE` clause - `'))--` or a similar payload terminates the query and returns all products.

`routes/showProductReviews.ts:36` builds a MarsDB query as `{ $where: 'this.product == ' + id }`, enabling JavaScript expression injection. The `id` is only truncated to 40 characters when the `noSqlCommandChallenge` is active; inserting `0;sleep(2000)` blocks the synchronous event loop.

**Relevant findings**

- 🟠 [F-001](#f-001) — NoSQL Injection in MarsDB Query Layer
- 🔴 [F-003](#f-003) — SQL injection
- 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — SQL Injection in Product Search

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - schema validation is present for email format and password length at registration but absent from numeric ID parameters and upload content paths.

`server.ts:407-418`; `models/user.ts:82-85`

`server.ts:407-418` validates the `email` field format on `POST /api/Users` and `models/user.ts:82-85` enforces minimum password length via Sequelize validators. No schema check covers numeric ID parameters on routes such as `routes/address.ts`, `routes/payment.ts`, or `routes/updateProductReviews.ts` - allowing string injection into ORM queries or MarsDB expressions. `routes/fileUpload.ts` enforces a file-size limit via `multer` but does not validate MIME type or archive structure before handing content to the XML, zip, or YAML parsers.
